# 6  
Predictive Analytics for Marketing and Sales of Products Using Smart Trolley with Automated Billing System in Shopping Malls Using LBPH and Faster R‐CNN

*Balla Adi Narayana Raju¹, Deepika Ghai¹, Suman Lata Tripathi¹, Sunpreet Kaur Nanda², and Sardar M.N. Islam³*

*¹ School of Electronics and Electrical Engineering, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, India*

*² Electronics and Telecommunication Engineering Department, P.R. Pote Patil College of Engineering and Management, Sant Gadge Baba Amravati University, Amravati, India*

*³ Institute for Sustainable Industries and Liveable Cities, Victoria University, Melbourne, Australia*

 

## 6.1 Introduction

Over the past decade, the internet and the economy have flourished simultaneously, leading to the birth of an era of hassle‐free shopping from the convenience of one’s living room. Internet technologies have always had the edge in tracking customers' purchase habits and exploiting them. However, traditional retail stores have not had a similar level of targeted advertisement and curated shopping experience \[[1](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0001), [2](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0002)\]. IoT technology in retail stores has remained relatively untouched by automation, image processing, and artificial intelligence (AI) advancements. Considering that supermarkets are where customers get their constant supply of daily use items, such as food products, garments, electronics devices, and household appliances \[[3](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0003), [4](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0004)\]; therefore, any amount of advancement based on customer preferences can cause a direct spike in the sales and perception of the product. Supermarket faces constant challenges like scanning many barcode items \[[2](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0002), [5](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0005)\], which increases the time and results in queuing delay as shown in [Figure 6.1](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0001). There is a need to reshape the billing process by implementing self‐service check‐outs. Recent advancements in chip technology have resulted in several realistic methods for new, hassle‐free applications [\[6\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0006). Barcode scanner takes a long time, but this can be avoided by attaching RFID tags to each item, which can then be scanned by RFID scanners that are both authentic and compact \[[4](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0004)–[7](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0007)\].

 ![Figure 6.1 Problems in supermarkets. ¶ Source: Yewatkar et al. \[4\]/Reproduced with permission from Elsevier.](images/c06f001.jpg)

[**Figure 6.1**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0001) Problems in supermarkets. ¶ *Source:* Yewatkar et al. [\[4\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0004)/Reproduced with permission from Elsevier.

 

## 6.2 Major Contributions

In this chapter, an efficient and effective trolley‐based model for monitoring the stores as well as the customers’ buying habits for a prediction of the items for better profitability is proposed. The proposed trolley is highlighted because it offers customer face recognition, automatic billing, customer tracking, and store classification. The smart trolley is built with the RFID reader, pi‐camera, ultrasonic sensors, ATmega328P controller, and Broadcom BCM2837 Advanced Risk Machine7 (ARM7) quad‐core processor as the main components \[[8](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0008), [9](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0009)\]. The customer in the store can scan each RFID tag on the item with an RFID reader and view information such as the product name and price along with the total balance on his/her mobile phone application. Customers have more flexibility since the bill is changed with each scanning of items and removed with scanned items. This trolley also helps customers to see their overall bill, which aids in budget control \[[10](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0010)–[12](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0012)\]. The management interface system (MIS) obtains information about the products and their costs, as well as alerts the store manager when new items need to be added to the racks on time. The face recognition used initially with the pi‐camera will detect the customer details and send it to MIS for a better shopping experience and interests to get customers’ buying patterns. Ultrasonic sensors HC‐SR04 used on either of the sides of the trolley will help in deciding the path according to the customer's movements for a hassle‐free experience \[[13](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0013)–[15](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0015)\]. Finally, people will have to pay their bills online instead of standing in long queues.

A unified trolley system is proposed in this chapter by combining IoT, image processing, ultrasonic sensing, and RFID technology. RFID has become a rapidly growing technology in economical industries for item‐level scanning \[[5](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0005), [16](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0016), [17](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0017)\]. In the coming days, these RFID tags are likely to be the most widely used. The 32‐bit ARM7 processor and ATmega328 controller are the core of the trolley, which interfaces with the MIS, mainframe, and mobile. These ARM7 and ATmega328 microcontrollers are the most used embedded system platforms due to their fast and efficient specifications. The proposed trolley aims to provide customers and store managers with a hassle‐free experience. This technology also provides a flexible, easily scalable, robust, cost‐effective, and technology‐oriented shopping experience, thereby reducing the queuing time.

The major challenges faced by the proposed trolley system in the supermarket setting for automation, detection, recognition, and analysis are the exertion of heavy loads on the self‐propelled system a heavy load‐hauling usually takes up high torque and overhauling could damage the motors. Also, the load in each cart is variable (this can though be accomplished with pulse width modulation \[PWM\]). So, managing the speed of the trolley is a major concern for automatic movement in the system.

The detection of the customer is also a major challenge \[[18](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0018)–[22](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0022)\]. Detecting the correct client and getting the data from the database to provide a better shopping experience accurately is concerning as errors caused by not recognizing the face properly due to the facial features can cause wrong recommendations. However, by using the best face recognition algorithms, facial analysis can be performed without any mismatch \[[23](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0023), [24](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0024)\].

The backgrounds in the real‐life scenarios are much more complex compared to the technical implementation datasets. Racks with items can be of uneven form thus being hard to detect and analyze if there are a large number of items placed. Training the convolutional neural network (CNN) with different store scenarios can reduce fault prediction \[[21](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0021)–[23](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0023)\].

Building up this kind of new framework and using it in the store will require a huge number of assets and information at the underlying stages to prepare the model. Heaps of testing stages need to be done to improve the procedure stream of the trolley from facial recognition, human following, and tag detection to monitoring the surroundings of the store \[[25](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0025)–[28](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0028)\]. Another challenge is making users take up these changes in a positive light, creating initial data that would help in future recommendations following principles of data security and privacy.

The rest of this chapter is organized as follows: [Section 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c6-sec-0002) represents the related work in the field of trolley‐based systems. [Section 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c6-sec-0003) provides the implementation details of the proposed method. [Section 6.4](#c06.xhtml#c6-sec-0004) discusses the results of various modules in the trolley. Finally, concluding remarks and promising research directions for the future are given in [Section 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c6-sec-0009).

 

## 6.3 Related Work

Researchers have proposed numerous methods for trolley‐based systems. In 2011, Agarwal et al. [\[6\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0006) built an RFID‐based automatic shopping cart that allows the customer a superior approach to shopping in‐store environment. This shopping trolley comprises a touch screen for the customer graphical interface attached to the cart, a client card, and an item reader for scanning the labels of the items placed in the cart, which makes it customer‐intuitive.

In 2013, McBeath [\[7\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0007) formulated that retailers need to adapt to another technology to persevere the complexity of items day by day. They require exact, real time, and item‐level pictures of stock. RFID has exhibited by improving store deals by 20–30%. JC Penney used RFID to increase stock accuracy from 75% to 99% in the gathering of products.

Kumar et al. [\[8\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0008) proposed an autonomous supermarket trolley system where RFID technology, ZigBee, and microcontrollers are used. This system has a start button for making the trolley active when an item with an RFID tag is placed in the trolley. The RFID reader reads the tag ID, and if the number matches the database, the product information tagged to that ID is displayed in the liquid crystal display (LCD). This display also has an end shopping button, when this button is pressed the total list of items present in the trolley is then sent to the master PC via ZigBee. There is also an option in the LCD to remove a particular item from the cart. This bill will be updated according to the customer's choice. The limitation of this system is that payments at the end are done on the billing counter for which the user should again waste sometime in the billing of items and the trolley is not smart enough to interact with the surroundings of the store.

In 2014, Kamble et al. [\[9\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0009) used RFID technology to build a multiprocessing shopping cart system. The item information is collected at the time of billing, and the RFID scanner is used to read it. This shopping cart system is made up of the following three parts: a display component and a user interface, a server communication component, and an automated billing component, all of which work together to create a smart trolley that significantly reduces billing time. Certain problems, such as stock maintenance, are not addressed in this scheme. That is if the substance that has been dropped in the trolley is not read and the required remedial action is not addressed.

Yewatkar et al. [\[4\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0004) discussed the components required for building a smart cart system with automatic billing, product information, and product recommendation using RFID, and ZigBee with anti‐theft using AT89S52 microcontroller, electrically erasable programmable read‐only memory (EEPROM), and LCD. The high data rate, voltages, communication protocol, and frequency requirements are shown, as well as the information on the inter‐integrated circuit (I2C) protocol between the microcontroller and EEPROM. Memory size and the need for more sophisticated communication protocols are both disadvantages of this configuration.

In 2015, Bhatwal et al. [\[10\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0010) proposed an automated self‐billing system using RFID technology which consists of a display panel, an RFID reader, and a master computer. The passive tags are initially scanned by the reader as shown in [Figure 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0002), which is then shown on the touch display panel if the user wants to remove the item then he/she can remove the item using the cancellation button in the display panel. The display panel also prints the bill at the end which the customer should pay using cash or card. The drawback of this system is the display panel that the author has described is a master computer which in reality, the billing process is complex.

 ![Figure 6.2 Working of RFID tag. ¶ Source: Bhatwal and Chamoli \[10\]/International Journal of Emerging Trends & Technology in Computer Science.](images/c06f002.jpg)

[**Figure 6.2**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0002) Working of RFID tag. ¶ *Source:* Bhatwal and Chamoli [\[10\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0010)/International Journal of Emerging Trends & Technology in Computer Science.

In 2016, Shraddha et al. [\[1\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0001) proposed an IoT‐based interactive trolley for shopping malls that uses RFID tags and includes an RFID reader, microcontroller, LCD, and an Espressif (ESP) module in the cart. The ESP offers real‐time details on what’s going on in the stores from anywhere. The major benefit of this system is there is no need for initial training.

Lee et al. [\[11\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0011) projected a mobile robot that can track and lead humans by using wearable devices to detect user position and behavior, as shown in [Figure 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0003). This chapter suggests the prototype of a small robot that can follow the user by using the smartwatch and belt to track the person using radio frequency (RF) communication with a low‐frequency (LF) transmitter. The drawback of this idea was that tracking or finding a person was difficult and was not so accurate.

![Figure 6.3 Smart mobile robot. ¶ Source: Lee et al. \[11\]/with permission of IEEE.](images/c06f003.jpg)

[**Figure 6.3**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0003) Smart mobile robot. ¶ *Source:* Lee et al. [\[11\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0011)/with permission of IEEE.

Chiang et al. [\[12\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0012) proposed a smart supermarket trolley that included a face recognition feature for store customers. The customers are also assisted with product recommendations based on their history of purchases. In this way, buying efficiency can be increased even further, and shopping time can be minimized. The algorithms for cart navigation and face recognition, on the contrary, are extremely complicated.

Wu et al. [\[13\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0013) suggested the concept of an intelligent shopping assistant system (ISAS) that could recognize and navigate the trolley consistently. Customers can see their shopping list and guide the shopping trolley to get items with this model’s additional app functionality. Customers can see their shopping list and direct the shopping trolley to get products using the app which is also an additional feature of this app. Product suggestions and discount details are also included in the app. This model has the benefit of providing a hands‐free shopping experience for the least amount of time spent at the shopping mall.

In 2018, Sarala et al. [\[29\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0029) designed a smart electronic trolley for shopping malls to reduce the time to purchase products. The barcode scanner on the electronic trolley scans the results to identify the items and connects to the shop's server over the Internet. In addition, it includes LCDs that inform customers of the number of items and their total cost and a barcode scanner that determines the result and updates the bill. A swiping machine is available so that customers can pay their bills using credit or debit cards.

Keerthana et al. [\[30\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0030) developed a system in which visually impaired people can enjoy their shopping experience and purchase the products they want. A specific personal gadget was designed for the identification of products and navigation. This project demonstrates how wireless personal gadgets are used in everyday life. It provides visually impaired people with a smart environment by using RFID and Zigbee technologies. The developed product enables visually impaired people to shop independently and not depend on others. You do not need any special training to use it, it is easy to use, and it is economical. The fully automated product overcomes the hesitation of purchasing their shopping needs and gives them confidence.

Devipriya et al. [\[31\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0031) stated that to enhance the quality of life for visually impaired and blind people, many methods have been proposed to date. However, purchasing products in a supermarket without others' assistance is still a big challenge for them. The purpose of this chapter is to propose a smart store assistant for visually impaired people. The assistant addresses three major concerns, namely, finding the product, identifying the product, and purchasing the product. The system has three modules: product identifier, smart glove, and smart trolley. RFID is used to identify products in the product identifier module. By scanning the product with the smart glove, the buyer receives audio instructions that explain the product specifications. As soon as the product is added to the cart, an automated billing system provides a final bill for the purchased products. An indoor navigation system will be developed in the future to locate the desired product from the user's location. This work demonstrates the design and implementation of a working prototype model. It is possible to improve or replace the low‐range RFID reader (EM‐18) in the present work with a high‐range RFID reader if this prototype is implemented in real time.

In 2019, Gunawan et al. [\[32\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0032) developed an automatic moving trolley, to solve the inconvenience and time wasted by customers looking for the desired product in the store. The purpose of a shopping trolley or shopping cart when shopping in a supermarket is to transport items to the cashier and not to take them outside. This chapter describes the hardware and software designs of a smart trolley system based on a two‐wheeled mobile robot developed previously. Various sensors, IOIO microcontrollers, and Android smartphones are used in this chapter. The Android smartphone controls the robot by sending a signal to the microcontroller paired with the actuator and monitors the situation with the camera of the smartphone. A Navisens indoor positioning system is also included in this system, which detects the user's location using the smartphone's accelerometer and gyroscope. The Navisens framework‐based smart trolley system can move and display its location to the user as the result of testing on robot navigation. Navisens provides a better indoor positioning system than any place because it updates its estimated position using the dead reckoning system, accelerometers, and gyroscopes instead of Wi‐Fi fingerprints. Despite being good at a distance of 1 and 3 meters, the smart trolley is not as stable at distances over 3 meters. It will be studied in the future whether the smart trolley system requires an automated calibrator to ensure the trolley does not depart from the path based on the analyses.

In 2020, Anitha et al. [\[33\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0033) stated that the objective of this chapter is to eliminate the problem of standing in line and wasting time while shopping. This study proposes a smart trolley billing system based on RFID tags that audits purchased products and makes payments online automatically. The cart identifies and scans products automatically, and the final billing is handled right there on the trolley. In addition, it provides customers with centralized and automated billing systems using RFID, making it a reasonable and profitable smart shopping cart. This article provides a time‐saving, commercially oriented, technology‐based system that enhances customer shopping experiences. The proposed system also suggested products based on user purchase history. With the proposed system, every product in the store should have an RFID tag, and every cart should have an RFID reader, which will save time and simplify shopping. This chapter has shown some challenges with smart shopping, i.e. sometimes items cannot be detected due to their tag orientations, sizes, and shapes. These interactions are usually quite expensive. These drawbacks have been addressed with this application. An LCD screen displays the automated billing amount on the smart trolley.

Das et al. [\[34\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0034) have proposed a smart trolley that handles both shopping and billing. Shopping can be thrilling and alluring, but it can also be time‐consuming and boring when you have to wait in long lines to pay your bill. With this system, the customer can walk into the shop right away, purchase products using the smart trolley, and walk out. He receives the e‐bill by mail and can view the details of his purchase online. To manage product and customer information, it requires an Arduino board, RFID reader, RFID tag, LCD, ESP8266 Wi‐Fi module, and a website that can be accessed anywhere in the world. This IOT‐based system allows the trolley to interact with a global network.

In 2021, Shailesh et al. [\[35\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0035) proposed a smart trolley for shopping. Weekends and holidays usually see a huge rush in markets, especially in metropolises. Huge discounts and offers make the rush even greater. We get a trolley in shopping malls in which we place all the items intended to be purchased from different shelves. After putting all the items into the trolley, we reach the counter for billing, leading to long queues. To solve this problem, a barcode reader and LCD screen are attached to the trolley. When the item is placed in the trolley, the barcode automatically detects the product's barcode and displays the item's details on a screen. The cost of the item is then added to the bill and the entire work is done with the help of Raspberry Pi. This practice will eliminate long queues at the counters because the trolley will do all the billing.

In 2022, Shete et al. [\[36\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0036) explained that not being able to see well or at all is just the tip of the iceberg for visually impaired people. Those groups face a whole host of societal challenges, stigmas, fears, and beliefs that make them the minority, and we do not give enough attention to what they face. It is incorrect to ignore the larger picture when chasing buzzwords like artificial intelligence and robotics to create complex technologies to solve mundane problems, but it is also essential to remember that mankind can use the bigger picture to save the rest of the planet. From navigating a path to shopping at the mall, those with visual impairments face a variety of difficulties. With braille‐assisted buttons and haptic feedback control, this chapter presents the idea of a smart shopping cart that helps visually impaired individuals navigate the various aisles of a shopping mall and purchase items without the need for human assistance.

Patil et al. [\[37\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0037) explained that there are several modern shopping malls (supermarkets) where we can purchase a wide range of goods, such as glass, toys, kitchen sets, groceries, and decorative items. Discounts, home delivery, and other factors make people shop. Due to busy schedules, boredom, and dissatisfaction with the services provided at the billing counter, customers have to wait in long lines, especially on weekends. For COVID‐19, we have implemented an effective method of social distancing to avoid these problems. As a solution to this unique customer problem, especially when it comes to billing, the system proposed is the Smart trolley, which scans items as they are put in, and displays the purchase amount and item in parallel on the LCD screen. It can scan a large number of items using RFID technology, saving both malls and consumers time.

Even though many researchers have proposed a large number of ideas \[[1](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0001), [4](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0004),[6](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0006)–[13](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0013),[29](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0029)–[37](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0037)\] a single unified approach needs to be done that fits all applications [\[38\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0038). In this chapter, a generic trolley‐based framework is proposed that uniformly describes the smart way of shopping in stores with a trolley interactive mechanism for showing the path and predicting the customer purchasing patterns.

 

## 6.4 Proposed Methodology

The trolley architecture comprises four modules as shown in [Figure 6.4](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0004). Each module has its advantages from getting customer details, and product details and classifying the store surroundings to the customer following. There are also image classification techniques used to monitor the surroundings for product identification and customer purchase pattern prediction for marketing strategies.

 

### 6.4.1 Module 1 – Face Recognition

Customer's faces are identified by the trolley using the camera module. The face is coordinated with the dataset of the store to check whether the face is present in the database or not. If there are matching facial characters found, then the trolley will be activated and the data corresponding to the application ID of the customer will be retrieved into the client’s application with a detailed chart of his/her interest and previous transactions. The client can also log in manually using his/her front‐facing camera, receiving a quick response (QR) that can be scanned at the trolley. In this event, if the face is not found in the dataset, at that point the face is added to the database (DB), and another ID is generated and activated with the new customer ID. The identification of the facial features has been done using a highly accurate face recognition algorithm, i.e. local binary pattern histogram (LBPH) of the OpenCV library to avoid the error associated with the detection of faces [\[14\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0014). [Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0005) shows the flowchart of the face recognition process.

The following are the stages involved in the face recognition system:

1.  **Image Acquisition:** It is where input images are gathered.
2.  **Face Detection:** The technology that identifies human faces in digital images is essentially computer technology that is used in a variety of applications.
3.  **Face Recognition:** It is a biometric technique for identifying an individual by comparing advanced image data to the person’s stored record.
4.  **Image Pre‐processing Techniques:** There is a need for preprocessing techniques to improve the quality of input images. The color‐to‐gray‐scale image conversion technique is used in this process, which converts the input image to a gray‐scale image. The mathematical representations for color‐to‐gray‐scale image conversion are given as follows:
    
    ![Figure 6.4 Flowchart of proposed trolley architecture. ¶ Source: Hayesphotography/Getty Images.](images/c06f004a.jpg)
    
    [**Figure 6.4**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0004) Flowchart of proposed trolley architecture. ¶ *Source:* Hayesphotography/Getty Images.
    
    1.  **Lightness:** (max (R, G, B + min (R, G, B))/3
    2.  **Average:** (R + G + B)/3
    3.  **Luminosity:** 0.21R +0.72G+ 0.07B
    
    where R, G, and B represent red, green, and blue components in an image.
    
5.  **Dataset:** To recognize an individual, the dataset includes an alternate number of images of his or her face.
6.  **Database:** Face recognition necessarily requires the comparison of face images with a database of images to be identified.
7.  **Training Set:** Face recognition is accomplished by matching the face to be identified with a database of recorded face appearances [\[15\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0015). We include the algorithm faces in the preparation set and identify which individual customers they belong to. When the calculation is required to recognize an unknown face, it uses a training package to do so.
    
    ![Figure 6.5 Flowchart of the face recognition process.](images/c06f005.jpg)
    
    [**Figure 6.5**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0005) Flowchart of the face recognition process.
    

There are three strategies for face recognition: Eigenfaces, Fisherfaces, and LBPH in the OpenCV library. Every one of the three referenced strategies utilizes a preparation set differently. Eigenfaces and Fisherfaces locate a scientific portrayal of the most prevailing highlights of the preparation set as a whole. Each face in the preparation set is independently and widely examined by LBPH. The LBPH algorithm used in this trolley system will not only detect the front faces but can also detect the side faces with 90% accuracy. For face detection of various input images, OpenCV with a Haar cascade classifier is used \[[16](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0016), [17](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0017)\]. The AdaBoost algorithm is used by the Haar cascade classifier to detect multiple face features of different customers, while the LBPH algorithm is used for recognition. The algorithm description for LBPH is as follows:

The LBPH algorithm uses the LBP operator for face extraction and it can be illustrated in [Figure 6.6](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0006). The formal description of the LBP operator is given in the equation ([Eq. \[6.1\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-disp-0002)).

![upper L upper B upper P left-parenthesis x Subscript s Baseline comma y Subscript s Baseline right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript n equals 0 Overscript n minus 1 Endscripts 2 Superscript n Baseline upper S left-parenthesis i Subscript n Baseline minus i Subscript s Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c6-disp-0001.png)<math display="block"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">LBP</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>s</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mstyle mathsize="140" displaystyle="true"><mo>&#8721;</mo></mstyle><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></munderover><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup><mi>S</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>&#8722;</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math> 

Here (*x*[*s*], *y*[*s*]) are central pixels with intensity *i*[*s*]; and *i*[*p*] is the intensity of the neighbor pixel. The sign function (*S*) is defined as:

[(6.1)](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-disp-0002)![upper S left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row 1st Column 1 2nd Column i f x greater-than-or-equal-to 0 2nd Row 1st Column 0 2nd Column e l s e EndLayout](images/c6-disp-0002.png)<math display="block"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mfenced><mi>x</mi></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{"><mrow><mtable equalrows="true" equalcolumns="true"><mtr><mtd><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">if</mi><mi mathvariant="normal"></mi><mi>x</mi><mo>&#8805;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd><mtd><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">else</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mfenced></mrow></math> 

The idea is to adjust a subjective number of neighbors on a hover with a variable radius, allowing for the capture of neighbor values as shown in [Figure 6.7](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0007).

For a given Point (*x*[*s*], *y*[*s*]) the position of the neighbor (*x*[*n*], *y*[*n*]), *n* ∈ *N* can be calculated by the equation ([Eq. \[6.2\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-disp-0004)).

![x Subscript n Baseline equals x Subscript s Baseline plus upper R cosine left-parenthesis StartFraction 2 pi n Over upper N EndFraction right-parenthesis](images/c6-disp-0003.png)<math display="block"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>s</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>cos</mi><mfenced><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>&#960;</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow><mi>N</mi></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></mrow></math> ![Figure 6.6 Original LBP operator.](images/c06f006.jpg)

[**Figure 6.6**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0006) Original LBP operator.

![Figure 6.7 Number of neighbors on a hover with a variable radius.](images/c06f007.jpg)

[**Figure 6.7**](#c06.xhtml#R_c6-fig-0007) Number of neighbors on a hover with a variable radius.

where *R* is the radius of the circle and *N* is the number of sample points.

This extended LBP operator is also referred to as a circular LBP operator. The point is interpolated if the coordinates of the points on the circle do not correspond to the image coordinates. The bilinear interpolation is implemented in OpenCV ([Eq. \[6.3\]](#c06.xhtml#c6-disp-0005))

The process flow of the face recognition module using Raspberry Pi Model B with Pi Camera v2 is shown in [Figure 6.8](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0008).

 

### 6.4.2 Module 2 – Trolley Movement

This module comprises sensors for making the trolley an independently moving user‐governed vehicle. The sensors have been set on either side of the trolley to act accordingly. The trolley movements are governed by the client's movements if the client is moving forward then the trolley follows while maintaining a certain separation between them automatically. Thus, hauling the check‐in products around the store makes the system simple to utilize, and the client can focus on the shopping experience better. The pin schematics of the trolley movement are shown in [Figure 6.9](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0009).

 

### 6.4.3 Module 3 – Items Detection

The client needs to pick the commodity that he/she is keen on and place it in the trolley. The RFID reader channels extract the label ID of each item and add it to the item information when the items are placed in the trolley. The details of the items are then sent to the central billing system of the market and subsequently to the cloud and the user's mobile. The aggregate sum will be paid using the client application on the mobile which is linked to the server. These passive tags are scanned more than once to remove the odd chance that there is any label that is not scanned. The client can incorporate the item using the application. In the end, all items are shown in the application as a summary to the client then the payment can be initiated online without the fuss created at the checkout counters. [Figure 6.10](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0010) shows the pin schematic of the RFID setup.

### 6.4.4 Module 4 – Object and Item Classification

This module contains a camera that is built into the trolley with numerous assignments and processes attached to it, from face acknowledgment to picture arrangement. The propelled grouping procedures and calculations are utilized to distinguish the items in the stores for better arrangement and for extracting the data utilizing different sorts of a classifier to anticipate the user or customer enthusiasm a better involvement in the store user. The process flow of object classification is as follows: (i) initially the various store images are labeled and trained for analyzing the store surroundings; (ii) real‐time images are acquired by the camera, which is then been pre‐processed; (iii) feature extraction of the images are done and passed to CNN; and (iv) classified result of output image has been seen with customers present in the particular aisle. Second, it can also anticipate the product details that have been held by the customers in a particular aisle. [Figure 6.11](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0011) shows the process flow of Faster R‐CNN.

 ![Figure 6.11 Process flow of Faster R‐CNN.](images/c06f011.jpg)

## 6.5 Experimental Results and Discussions

 

### 6.5.1 Experimental Results

This section shows the results of each module.

*   **Module 1 – Face Recognition:** Datasets are taken by detecting the faces of the customers and each user or customer is given a unique identification number as shown in [Figure 6.12](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0012). The trainer is trained in a way that each identity of the customer is been replaced by a customer database and recognized. Haar classifier is used to detect the face features, and LBPH is used for recognizing the existing customer, as shown in [Figure 6.13](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0013).
*   **Module 2 – Trolley Movement:** The customer's walking movements are detected by the trolley using ultrasonic sensors connected to the Arduino Uno board. The range from customer and trolley is calculated by sensors and preprocessed to act accordingly. The logic made her is if the person moves forward the trolley moves forward and simultaneously if the customer moves backward the trolley also moves backward as shown in [Figure 6.14](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0014).
*   **Module 3 – Items Detection:** The RFID reader (RC522) connected to the trolley will help it in scanning store products and extracting the product ID with information as well as an aggregate amount for payment as shown in [Figure 6.15](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0015).
*   **Module 4 – Object and Item Classification:** The implementation of the product identification is done by deep CNN algorithms like Faster R‐CNN. Various deep‐learning Python libraries are used for different purposes in the algorithms. The integrated development environment (i.e. Spyder) is used for the development of the algorithm. Python 3.5 programming language is used for the development of the program. The store item images are gathered and then converted to an XML file using a labeling tool. The labeled images are then given to the CNN for training. The output result after the network is fully trained is shown in [Figure 6.16](#c06.xhtml#c6-fig-0016). Training in the simulator is offline training that occurs in an anaconda simulator, which is very useful for debugging and allowing the robot to walk around in an environment without fear of damaging it or its batteries. First and foremost, the initial training images are identified by finding store images in the surrounding area at random. According to the training conditions, it can manage more difficult circumstances in this scenario.

In a real‐time scenario, each trolley has a unique identification number, which will assist the customer so that during the billing, no customer faces the problem.

 

### 6.5.2 Discussions on the Security Aspect of IoT

Nowadays, most of the community uses wireless networks, i.e. Wi‐Fi, Zigbee, GSM, etc., for automation and live monitoring. But, IoT systems threaten to hack the system. Therefore, the following safety measures are preferable to secure the use of IoT modules in real time \[[1](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0001), [25](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0025),[37](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0037)–[40](#c06.xhtml#c6-bib-0040)\].

1.  The users should not share and store personal data and passwords on the cloud. The users use the authenticated website for installing the software.
2.  The user should not install any malware without authenticity.
3.  Switch off the Wi‐Fi network and IoT network when a user does not require it because smartphone‐based apps are more vulnerable.
4.  IoT service providers should use authenticate software for intelligent device applications.
5.  Regular software patches for smartwatches, IoT sensors, IoT gadgets, healthcare apps on smartphones, and other IoT devices should be provided by the IoT service provider. This improves the security of IoT devices. These fixes should be capable of dealing with the most recent malware and viruses.
6.  House owners should exercise caution to ensure that no unclaimed IoT gadget is installed or placed on their property without their permission. Later on, hackers may be able to use these devices for malicious purposes.

 

## 6.6 Conclusion

Today, shopping for a day in malls or supermarkets is a daily activity in cities. On weekends and special days with sales, one can see a huge rush at malls. People buy various items and put them in the trolley and go to the payment counter. The cashier uses a bar code reader machine to scan the bill at the billing counter. This chapter aims to develop an automated trolley system for smart shopping and billing that can be used to minimize manpower shortages at the billing counter. The main idea here is to facilitate a customer with reduced time spent buying a product in daily shopping. This proposed model is equipped with four modules: (i) face recognition by using LBPH for getting customer details; (ii) user walks independently and prevents collisions with other bodies at the same time by using ultrasonic sensors connected to Arduino Uno board; (iii) Scanning and calculating cart products using RFID tags attached to items and sending data using IoT technology for convenience billing and store alerts on inventory; and (iv) object and item classification for detecting the surrounding store aisle.

# 7  
Enhancing Stock Market Predictions Through Predictive Analytics

*Ameya Patil¹, Shantanu Saha ², and Rajeev Sengupta ³*

*¹ Faculty of School of Business, MIT World Peace University, Pune, India*

*² Faculty of Ramcharan School of Leadership, MIT World Peace University, Pune, India*

*³ Faculty of School of Business, Dr. Vishwanath Karad MIT World Peace University, Pune, India*

 

## 7.1 Introduction

The stock market is a gathering of buyers and sellers and provides a venue for trading the shares of various corporations. Financial markets assist economic growth by facilitating international trade, gathering, discounting, and disseminating information about organizations' and economies' future prospects. Financial securities amounting to trillions of dollars are traded daily on the stock exchanges around the world. With the advent of technology and popularization of online trading applications, such as Zerodha and Upstox providing convenience, interest of common people has also grown in stock markets. In emerging countries like India, many were attracted to stock markets during the Covid‐19 pandemic. This was evident by the record number of new demat accounts opened on online platforms such as Zerodha in 2020. Stock market forecasting has long been a difficult and perplexing issue, which has caught the attention of the general public and, the professionals in this area, and of experts like economists and computer scientists. Successfully anticipating stock price movements can result in alluring rewards through strategic action, making stock market trend prediction a task worthy of significant focus and effort. Stock market forecasting is a very difficult undertaking, and various aspects must be addressed to make effective market predictions. Considering the fact that stock price is dependent on numerous aspects, only taking into account one or two criteria will not be sufficient to provide adequate information to estimate the stock price movement. Hence, it is appropriate to include as much pertinent and helpful data as possible to ensure a more accurate estimate. Profits are realized by those investors who make the right buy and sell choices. The stock market prediction aimed at analyzing the trends is convoluted because the stock market is inherently noisy and has a lot of volatility when it comes to trends [\[1\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0001). Several strategies have been developed in existing methodologies to predict stock market trends. The reliability of financial market forecasting and the viability of lucrative model‐based trading are heavily impacted by a gamut of factors. These include the market's maturity, the forecasting method utilized, the time frame for which forecasts are made, as well as the methodology employed to evaluate the model and simulate trade [\[2\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0002).

The efforts to stock market prediction begin with fundamental analysis and technical analysis. Fundamental analysis is a sort of investment analysis wherein the company’s past and estimated sales, profits, and other financial parameters are analyzed along with other economic based on factors that affect the value of the company. This type of analysis assesses the stock price in light of its intrinsic value, viz., fair value. This approach is best suited for long‐term forecasting. Technical analysis is based on charts and trends. This analysis makes use of historical stock prices to forecast price trends. The input features for machine learning (ML) and AI models can also be employed with experience‐based technical indicators. The fundamental idea of ML is to draw knowledge through data. Next, time series forecasting using linear models for stock market forecasting is in vogue, including ARIMA and GARCH. The 21st century was defined by the rapid development of electronic markets and the emergence of computer‐driven high‐frequency trading enabled by computing technologies [\[3\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0003). Nonlinear models such as artificial neural networks (ANNs) have then been used to overcome the limitations of linear models, considering the chaotic nature of stock markets. Utilizing time series analysis technology for stock forecasting is vital for creating stock trading strategies and earning greater trading returns [\[4\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0004). ML techniques for predicting the stock market are in vogue. Numerous studies that utilized ML for financial time series forecasting appear to be able to predict future market events with up to 80% accuracy [\[2\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0002). Analysts and investors place a premium on the ability to anticipate shifts in stock prices. The challenge of making accurate and efficient stock market predictions is quite complex, and various elements should be taken into account. Stock prices respond to market changes, quarterly earnings announcements, and evolving consumer patterns. Traders utilize a variety of technical indicators derived from daily stock data.

The purpose of this chapter is to provide a thorough examination of several stock prediction approaches for forecasting future trends and stock returns. This chapter takes into account a number of stock market forecasting methods that have been used in previous research studies.

 

## 7.2 Factors Influencing Stock Prices

Earnings in the stock market can be forecasted using traditional methods based on past stock returns, as well as financial and economic indicators. The forecast of stock market returns led investors to investigate the grounds of predictability. The variety of variables that can be used to make forecasts should be taken into consideration [\[5\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0005). Forecasting stock trends is a difficult technique since it is dependent on a variety of factors, such as the specific market patterns, expectations of investors and traders, and their financial circumstances. Several news and events also impact stock values. Furthermore, the stock price list is by definition dynamic, complicated, noisy, nonparametric, and nonlinear. Financial time series forecasting is challenging due to a variety of complicated properties such as volatility, anomalies, noise, and shifting trends. Enormous amounts of data regarding stocks are generated on a daily basis and are accessible from various channels such as stock exchange websites, economic reviews, company reports. Different research studies utilized distinct sets of factors as input to their prediction models, as no consensus regarding all aspects crucial to stock market forecasting exists [\[6\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0006).

The following data have been so far used to predict stock prices:

1.  **Market Data:** Market data refers to the collection of information and statistics related to financial markets and the trading activities that take place within them. It includes various types of data that provide insights into the prices and volumes. Also included are the current market price, opening price, closing price, high and low prices for a given time period.
2.  **Text Data:** Individually written text from sources, such as news, social media, and web searches is referred to as text data. As an alternate data source, these statistics are difficult to acquire and evaluate. However, they can offer valuable insights that are not reflected in aggregate market data. However, they may provide helpful information that is not reflected in aggregate market data. A sentiment component can be added to text data through the use of sentiment analysis (such as positive, negative, or neural). The sentiment component is then applied to prediction.
3.  **Macroeconomics Numbers:** Macroeconomic indicators, such as the gross domestic product (GDP), inflation rates, interest rates, reveal the economic realities of a particular nation, area, or sector. These indicators have some connection to the stock market because they reflect the market as a whole and can verify the validity of a stock price increase or decrease.
4.  **Knowledge Graph Data:** Different companies and markets interact in some way. For instance, similar news may influence the movement of stocks in the same industry. Knowledge graph data from open sources can be leveraged to enhance prediction accuracy utilizing graph neural networks.
5.  **Image Data:** Image data can be used to create visualizations of stock market data, such as candlestick and line charts. Some researchers have investigated using satellite imagery or aerial images to gather information related to economic activity, such as analyzing parking lot occupancy rates or estimating oil storage levels, which could indirectly impact certain stocks or sectors.
6.  **Fundamental Data:** Accounting data, such as assets and liabilities, is the most typical sort of fundamental data, which is reported quarterly. Financial information of a corporation, such as its sales, earnings, assets, liabilities, and other financial ratios, comprise fundamental data. Fundamental data reveals a company's financial health and can influence its stock price.
7.  **Recommendation Data:** Analyst recommendation data refers to information provided by financial analysts or brokerage companies regarding their opinions and recommendations regarding particular stocks or investment opportunities. Analysts do research and analysis on companies and industries, and issue reports including buy, sell, or hold recommendations for specific stocks based on their findings.

 

## 7.3 Can Markets Be Predicted?

The efficient markets hypothesis (EMH), a central tenet of finance theories, holds that market price represents all available information. As a result, risk‐free profits cannot be achieved [\[7\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0007). According to EMH, as market prices vary in a random manner, future values cannot be predicted using data from the past. EMH may be broken if an investor can consistently outperform the market in terms of risk‐adjusted return. Despite being prevalent, this hypothesis is not widely accepted since it cannot account for a number of minor but major instances of market inefficiencies. These anomalies include higher returns posted by institutional investors, the market’s over‐reaction and under‐reaction, and high volatility of asset prices.

 

### 7.3.1 Different Stock Prediction Techniques

Many forecast models presume that the past may be utilized to envisage future stock market movements. Several ML and econometric methods have been used in stock market forecasting. Financial time series forecasting, frequent use of ML technologies, claims that sophisticated forecasting algorithms effectively predict price movements in stock markets, and trading on these predictions can generate a profit. Financial economists, on the contrary, refer to the informational efficiency of financial markets, which calls into question price prediction and prospects for lucrative trading [\[2\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0002). Several research studies have utilized a combination of two or more methods collectively to forecast the stock markets. A combination of fundamental data and technical indicators has been used in many research works.

*   **Fundamental Analysis:** In this type of analysis, underlying elements that affect businesses or sectors are used as predictive attributes [\[8\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0008). Fundamental analysis focuses on fundamental information, mainly contained in the annual reports of companies, such as a company’s sales and profits, the annual growth rate in various financial parameters, company’s position in the market and its future prospects [\[9\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0009). One of the important aspects of fundamental analysis is economic analysis, which focusses on the macroeconomic parameters such as national income, exports data and interest rates which ought to impact the operations of companies listed on a stock exchange. In addition, sectoral information, which ought to impact stocks belonging to specific sectors, is also incorporated into fundamental analysis. Using this information, the company’s future cash flows and thereby, the company’s value is arrived at, and is used to forecast its stock price. Early studies on stock market prediction have shown an interest in fundamental indicators and related variables \[[10](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0010), [11](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0011)\]. Fundamental analysis, when incorporated with ANNs can result in superior returns \[[12](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0012), [13](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0013)\].
*   **Technical Analysis:** Technical analysis refers to assessment of previous stock price and volume information to forecast stock price changes [\[14\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0014). Technical analysis is based on analyzing the current demand–supply of the tradable instrument. A variety of technical indicators are used to generate “buy” and “sell” signals. The majority of research works regarding stock price prediction through ML techniques have employed technical indicators [\[15\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0015). Technical analysis and historical market movements have the ability to anticipate daily returns in an active trading environment [\[16\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0016). Technical indicators like moving averages, oscillators, and trend lines are used. Several research studies have employed technical indicators as input for forecasting stock markets \[[17](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0017), [18](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0018)\]. In the age of AI, technical indicators have been provided as inputs to a neural network to improve prediction accuracy \[[19](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0019), [20](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0020)\].
*   **Sentiment Analysis:** With the easy availability of the Internet globally, internet and social media disperse huge amount of information related to stocks as well as facilitate users to engage in expressing their opinion related to the same. The sentiment analysis approach involves analyzing investor sentiment and market psychology to gauge market direction. Sentiment analysis includes monitoring news, social media, and market indicators to assess the overall mood and sentiment of market participants. Incorporating certain public mood dimensions can improve the prediction accuracy regarding stock markets [\[21\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0021). When applied correctly, investments assisted by sentiment analysis tools can yield rewards. Together, sentiment analysis and collective intelligence can automatically compute the stock market's future performance [\[22\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0022). Feature extraction of social media text data can describe the emotional tendencies of investors and enhance the precision of stock price forecasting [\[23\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0023). The addition of news and sentiments can improve deep learning models' performance as regards to accuracy in price prediction. Textual information in news headlines is related to stock price forecasting \[[24](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0024), [25](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0025)\] used technical indicators from stock prices and sentiments from textual news articles to build a layered deep learning neural network model prediction model, which brings more prediction enhancements.

 

## 7.4 Using Predictive Analytics for Stock Prediction

A data‐driven methodology called predictive analytics combines statistical algorithms and historical data to forecast upcoming events or results. Predictive analytics is the process of creating algorithms that can anticipate future stock prices or locate prospective trading opportunities using previous stock market data. Enormous amount of structured and unstructured heterogeneous data is generated related to stock markets and the individual scrips therein, can be effectively analyzed through advanced ANNs. ML and AI are extensively used when it comes to predictive analytics.

Here are some typical steps in the stock prediction process utilizing predictive analytics:

1.  **Data Collection:** Collect historical data for the stock(s) you wish to examine. This often comprises daily or intraday pricing data, trade volumes, and other pertinent information such as financial statements or economic indicators. In addition, for a more comprehensive study, company‐specific data and news sentiment data can be included.
2.  **Data Preprocessing:** Clean and preprocess the data by dealing with missing values, outliers, and, if necessary, normalizing the data. This phase ensures that the data is in an appropriate format for analysis.
3.  **Feature Selection:** Determine the most important traits or elements that may have an impact on stock pricing. Technical indicators, financial ratios, economic indicators, and news mood ratings are examples. Regarding feature selection, some researchers investigate the relationship between stock prices and certain novel variables, such as political considerations, macroeconomic factors, and investor sentiment, among others, and include them in the prediction model.
4.  **Model Selection:** Based on your facts and goals, select a suitable predictive model. Regression methods, time series models such as ARIMA, GARCH, or ML algorithms, or neural networks are generally used for stock prediction.
5.  **Training the Model:** Divide the historical data into two sets: training and testing. Use a training set for training the predictive model by fitting it to the historical data, adjusting model parameters, and optimizing performance.
6.  **Evaluation and Tuning:** Using the testing set, evaluate the predictive model's performance. Mean squared error (MSE), root mean squared error (RMSE), accuracy, and precision‐recall measurements are common stock prediction evaluation metrics. If the model's performance is poor, hyperparameter tuning and model tweaks can be made to increase accuracy.
7.  **Prediction and Decision Making:** Once the model has been trained and evaluated, use it to forecast future stock prices or find potential trading opportunities based on new data. Monitor and analyze the expected results to determine the model's effectiveness and, if necessary, adjust the method.

 

### 7.4.1 Stock Prediction Using ML and AI

Experts have long stressed the need for prediction, especially as the environment has gotten more chaotic and turbulent. Information technology advancements for forecasting can be aided by information about the necessary set of indicators. Forecasts, on the contrary, are based on previous events and cannot easily incorporate all essential information, particularly unstructured information. The combination of AI and data analytics in forecasting would surely benefit investors.

Because of the irregularity of the financial market, simplistic models cannot accurately forecast future asset values [\[26\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0026). Hence, some investors and institutions use ML and AI algorithms to find patterns or trends in massive amounts of data. These techniques can help in generating predictions or identifying potential trading opportunities based on historical data. Many ML models have shown the value of a novel ML model for time series forecasting utilizing financial market data such as stock prices, currency rates, or financial indices. They have also identified profitable trading opportunities based on model forecasts. It has been observed that ML algorithms perform better than most traditional stochastic methods for predicting financial markets [\[27\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0027).

 

## 7.5 Neural Networks

Neural networks, as a wise information mining approach and profound learning methods, are useful in learning complicated forms of information by applying regulated learning models, where it gradually learns from datasets and experience. Neural networks provide a revolutionary technique that does not necessitate pre‐specification during the modeling phase because they understand the link inherent in the variables on their own. This is particularly beneficial in the security investing arena, where much is assumed, but little is understood about the processes that determine stock prices. Due to their aptitude for learning nonlinear mappings [\[28\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0028) between input and output, neural networks are used to forecast stock market prices [\[29\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0029). Because of the large amounts of information generated in capital markets, machines would master many designs, allowing them to make reasonable forecasts [\[30\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0030). Neural networks have seen widespread use for stock prediction owing to their capability to learn intricate patterns and relationships in data.

 

### 7.5.1 Artificial Neural Network

ANN is an algorithm meant to comprehend complex problems that cannot be addressed by standard ML methods or accessible neural networks. ANNs have transferred human learning ability from their experiences into a computer environment that can mimic the working principles of the human brain. ANNs are constructed with a more intricate network of interconnections than the human brain [\[31\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0031). The approach uses algebraic equations to direct data to a model or time series line. By means of a self‐learning approach, ANN can discover hidden features. These are good approximators that can find input and output relationships of a huge complex dataset. Hence, ANN is a suitable alternative for predicting stock prices. ANN is utilized to reveal the complex correlations between stock performance and related financial and technical variables [\[32\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0032). Typically, financial variables serve as the inputs and stock performance is the resultant output, for ANN applications. Numerous research studies have revealed the existence of nonlinearity in financial markets and the applicability of ANN to uncover this relationship. ANN has been widely utilized to achieve accurate stock market forecasting \[[1](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0001), [32](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0032)\]. Several research works have revealed that ANNs outperformed statistical models like ARIMA [\[33\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0033). At the same time, the combination of statistics and learning models has polished neural networks. The input variables and network parameters for ANNs must be carefully chosen. The ability to accurately predict the future is highly dependent on learning algorithm employed during ANN training \[[34](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0034), [35](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0035)\] found FNN and RNN types, such as LSTM, GRU, and CNN, quite effective in predicting the riskiness of Indian stock market indicated by the fear factor called volatility index (VIX).

ANNs are neural networks with one or more layers that are fully interconnected. A sample ANN comprising input layer, output layer, and three hidden layers is shown below ([Figure 7.1](#c07.xhtml#c7-fig-0001)). Every node in a layer is connected to each node in the layer above it. It is feasible to make the network deeper by increasing hidden layers.

The backpropagation (BP) algorithm is a widely used optimization technique to train ANNs. BP is a supervised learning algorithm used to adjust the weights and biases of neural networks during the training process, aiming to minimize the difference between predicted and actual outputs. In majority of the related researches, BP algorithm has been used widely, especially for training the data set. However, owing to the high levels of noise and complicated dimensionality in stock market data, ANN has some constraints when learning patterns. Hence, many research studies have applied hybrid models, wherein the traditional BP algorithm has been used in conjunction with other algorithms. Hybrid models of ANN and genetic algorithms (GAs) for feature discretization have been widely used in this context. The classical BP learning algorithm, when aided by global search techniques such as GA and simulated annealing (SA), can significantly increase prediction accuracy [\[36\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0036). Modifications can be made to neural networks using enhanced algorithms such as the improved sparrow algorithm. Utilizing it for tuning initial weight as well as threshold of BP neural network resulted in a highly accurate short‐term forecast of stock values [\[37\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0037). Improved sine cosine algorithm (ISCA) combined with BP neural networks, along with Google Trends, achieved prediction accuracy to the tune of 87% [\[38\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0038). Neural networks when assisted using nonlinear independent component analysis (NLICA) for feature extraction improves the prediction accuracy [\[39\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0039).

![Figure 7.1 A sample of artificial neural network.](images/c07f001.jpg)

[**Figure 7.1**](#c07.xhtml#R_c7-fig-0001) A sample of artificial neural network.

 

#### 7.5.1.1 Recurrent Neural Network (RNN)

Depending upon their architecture and purpose, there are numerous ANN kinds. Feedforward neural network (FNN) is a basic type of ANN, where information flows unidirectionally, viz., from the input layer to the output layer. FNN’s take historical stock price information, along with other pertinent features such as trading volume, technical indicators, or economic data, as input and predict future stock prices or market trends. Stock market prediction studies have mainly used RNN, a neural network architecture designed to handle sequential data by considering the temporal aspect of the information. The main characteristic of RNN is its knack to capture and propagate information from previous time steps to current time step, allowing them to model dependencies and patterns in sequential data. When it comes to stock prediction, RNNs can be used to process historical stock price data and other relevant features over time, capturing patterns and relationships that can aid in forecasting stock prices.

The RNN architecture known as long short‐term memory (LSTM) is particularly good at identifying long‐term dependencies and patterns in sequential data. LSTM networks consist of memory cells that store and update information over time, as well as gates that regulate the flow of information through the network. The three gates that make up an LSTM are input gate, forgetting gate, and output gate. Information not complying with the algorithm will be erased by the forgetting gate. Hence, inly the compliant data remains [\[40\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0040). For the purposes of forecasting stock prices, LSTM networks can be trained to learn patterns and relationships in historical stock price data, as well as other relevant features. By considering the sequential nature of the data and incorporating memory cells, LSTM networks can potentially capture trends, seasonality, and other patterns that are significant for predicting future stock prices.

LSTM basically operates as a three‐step process. In the first step, the LSTM is to decide which information to be omitted from the cell in that particular time step. This decision is made with the help of the sigmoid function, which computes the function using the prior state and the current input. The second layer contains two functions, viz., the sigmoid function and the tanh function. The sigmoid function determines which values are allowed to pass (0 or 1). Tanh function assigns weightage to the values passed, deciding their level of importance from −1 to 1. The third stage determines the ultimate result. First, a sigmoid layer needs to be run, which determines what parts of the cell state make it to the output. The cell state is then passed to the tanh function, which pushes values between −1 and 1 and multiplies it by sigmoid gate output.

Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) can be applied to certain aspects of stock prediction. CNN is a feed‐forward neural network. More hidden layers are present in CNN vis‐a‐vis traditional neural networks. Designing CNN's architecture for stock prediction challenges is conceivably a key problem that merits additional research. Primarily, CNN can be employed for data visualization purposes, converting financial data into visual representations such as candlestick charts, market depth plots, or heat maps. Such visualizations can help traders and analysts gain insights into market trends and patterns. Second, CNNs can potentially be used for feature extraction and preprocessing of financial time series data. Next, CNN can also be used in sentiment analysis by processing textual data such as news articles, social media posts, or financial reports related to the stock market. CNN can also analyze alternative data sources, such as satellite imagery, web‐scraped data, or visual data from sensors. A deep CNN‐based framework applied to extract higher‐level features from a large number of financial variables can significantly improve prediction accuracy [\[5\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0005).

RNNs are hampered by the vanishing gradient problem [\[41\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0041), which makes it difficult for them to recognize long‐term dependencies in sequential data. Another RNN called gated recurrent unit (GRU) is employed to solve this problem. The primary characteristic of a GRU is the incorporation of gating mechanisms that allow the network to control the flow of information across time, hence enabling the network to retain and update pertinent information over longer sequences. By means of gating mechanisms, GRUs excel in capturing long‐range dependencies in sequential data, making them better suited for applications where long‐term information retention is critical. Unlike the LSTM, where the output gate controls what content is seen by the other units, the GRU does not have controlled memory exposure. GRUs can be used for time series forecasting, where historical stock prices and other pertinent information are fed into the network, and the model predicts future stock prices or market trends. GRUs' capacity to detect temporal dependencies in the data may be useful for spotting patterns and trends in stock price movements. By learning meaningful representations of input data, the model can capture complex patterns that may impact stock prices. In addition, GRUs can extract features from time series data. The time series events in input may be successfully captured by LSTM and GRU models. In addition, a fully connected neural network is utilized to combine numerous independent prediction outcomes to increase prediction accuracy [\[42\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0042). GRUs can be employed for sentiment analysis using textual data like financial news articles or social media posts related to the stock market. GRU model fed with historical prices and the sentiment score delivered great prediction results [\[24\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0024). Incorporating financial news sentiments along with stock attributes as the input can considerably improve the performance of LSTM and GRU [\[43\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0043). Deep learning models which integrate multiple several CNN pipelines along with unidirectional or bidirectional GRUs have been instrumental in enhancing stock prediction [\[44\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0044). One of the variants, nested GRU (NGRU) employs a nested structure to boost model depth and eliminate noise from stock data features. Learning the features of stock data, NGRU has been found to be more sensitive and effective.

 

#### 7.5.1.2 Deep Learning Models

Deep learning is focused on building multilayered ANNs. Deep learning approaches use data to tackle complex problems with fewer assumptions. Deep learning automatically extracts features from data, requiring little to no human involvement in feature selection. The term "deep" alludes to the numerous layers that make up the neural network architecture. Deep learning employs neural networks with multiple hidden layers, allowing the network to learn representations of the data at different levels of abstraction. Each layer in the network extracts higher‐level features from the representations learned by the previous layers, thus creating a hierarchy of learned features. Deep neural networks integrate benefits of deep learning and neural networks. Hence, they can be used to address nonlinear problems more effectively than traditional ML algorithms [\[45\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0045). Such networks yield greater precision with less human effort and minimal tuning. Deep learning has been applied to stock prediction, particularly in the context of time series analysis, because of its ability to understand complicated patterns and representations from past data. Deep learning models need massive input data to build a complicated neural network model [\[46\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0046). Here, market data is the best choice and is the most popular. Text data is the second most used type. This is because social media and online news sites are so popular, and it is easy to get this type of data. The majority of deep learning systems produce forecasts based on previous stock price, volume, or derivative data [\[4\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0004). Deep learning may be appealing for stock market prediction at high frequencies since it can extract characteristics from a large amount of raw data without relying on prior knowledge of predictors [\[47\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0047). Deep learning methods come in a wide variety of network structures, activation functions, and other model parameters, and it is well‐recognized that the way data is represented has a significant impact on how well these algorithms perform. According to empirical findings, deep neural networks can improve prediction accuracy by extracting additional data from autoregressive model residuals.

 

## 7.6 Conclusion

Stock market forecasting has been an area of great interest to the practitioners as well as the investors. However, this task is difficult since stock markets are complex, volatile, and nonlinear [\[41\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0041). The early studies in stock market forecasts were mainly in fundamental analysis [\[10\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0010) and technical analysis [\[17\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0017). With the advent of AI, the various variables used in technical and fundamental analysis are feed as inputs to the ANNs \[[4](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0004), [20](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0020)\]. Such studies have been widely used in stock market forecast, along with time series forecasting. Also, news articles and sentiment analysis data are being incorporated into neural networks for stock price prediction [\[45\]](#c07.xhtml#c7-bib-0045). ANNs have also seen development in terms of improvements in the existing technology. Several research studies have used hybrid models for stock forecasting. The recurrent neural networks especially LSTM, GRU, and CNN are now being widely used along with some permutations and combination of these networks. The traditional back propagation algorithm used in neural networks has been added by several other algorithms to improve prediction accuracies. Several research studies have fine‐tuned and developed ANN to improve prediction accuracy, and the efforts in this direction are in full swing. The accuracy of predictions can be increased by combining several approaches, employing boosting algorithms, and including feature selection. Stock prediction will always be an area of research and will be defined by the fundamental data and technical indicators, financial news and sentiments, the advent of technologies such as hybrid ANNs, as well as the algorithms used therein for training and prediction purposes. The future stock prediction techniques should take into account a myriad factor affecting stock markets, including sentiments, political news, government policies, and others. Hence, the stock prediction models developed in the future should incorporate data from different sources. Furthermore, ongoing advances in artificial intelligence and ML algorithms should be leveraged to create models that are adaptive, capable of learning from new data and better equipped to deal with unexpected market shifts.
