# 10  
A Simple Way to Comprehend the Difference and the Significance of Artificial Intelligence in Agriculture

*Karan Aggarwal ¹, Ruchi Doshi ², Maad M. Mijwil ³, Kamal Kant Hiran⁴, Murat Gök ⁵, and Indu Bala⁶*

*¹ Electronics and Communication Engineering Department, Maharishi Markandeshwar (Deemed to be University), Ambala, India*

*² Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Universidad Azteca, Chalco, Mexico*

*³ Computer Techniques Engineering Department, Baghdad College of Economic Sciences University, Baghdad, Iraq*

*⁴ School of Computer Science and IT, Symbiosis University of Applied Sciences, Indore, India*

*⁵ Department of Computer Engineering, Yalova University, Yalova, Turkey*

*⁶ School of Electrical and Electronics Engineering, Lovely Professional University, Punjab, India*

 

## 10.1 Introduction

At this time, the devices technology revolution is developing day by day. We live in the era of devices that help us accomplish many tasks in our lives. Technology allows solving people's economic situations unprecedentedly that they would not have dreamed of 20 years ago. In computer science, the science of artificial intelligence (AI) appeared, which is an essential part of it, and also the science that is more developed and applied in many fields, including the medical domain, military, people tracking, social networking sites, and many endless applications \[[1](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0001), [2](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0002)\]. The science of AI can be represented as the ability of a machine or a computer to execute tasks related to the human mind, such as thinking, reasoning, generalization, and learning from past things and experiences to perform functions that are greater than or equal to the ability of humans to accomplish [\[3\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0003). In 1956, a concept appeared that changed many ideas in computer science which is AI, which was mentioned at the Dartmouth conference. In this event, we discussed how to realize and support computer software intelligently. The term AI did not meet the purpose of some of the scientists present, including Newell and Simon, and at the same time, John McCarthy, Marvin Minsky, and Claude Shannon, who helped a lot in building the idea of AI, approved it. The idea of creating robots is not new, as some think. On the contrary, it is an ancient idea that dates back many years. Nevertheless, examples exist of the concept of giving life to inanimate objects and transforming them into intelligent and efficient objects. In Greek myths, Daedalus' attempt to produce artificial humans who ruled the wind can be considered the first example of AI. In general, scientists always seek to develop machines based on the idea of AI by linking computers with intelligence \[[4](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0004), [5](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0005)\]. The most significant example of this is the Turing machine (*the enigma machine*) in 1943 by the English mathematician Alan Turing.

Turing claimed in 1950, in his famous article “Computing Machinery and Intelligence”, that computers could calculate and develop a test to decide whether or not a machine is intelligent [\[6\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0006). After 1960, many studies described the difference between the human brain and the computer's ability to control things. On this basis, engineering and cognitive modeling were designed and narrowed the gap between man and machine, which is still continuing until now. However, man is the engineer of machines and lays the basis for their assignment and programming in the way they wish. But the most critical question is whether computers will have the ability to think uniquely like humans and operate without the intervention of any human hand. In other words, can human consciousness as a biological being be transferred to a machine? There are many inquiries about the future of AI technologies [\[7\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0007).

Today, we are witnessing a lot of articles and research that talk about the integration of computers into our future lives \[[8](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0008), [9](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0009)\]. Also, AI has a significant role in confronting diseases, especially the COVID‐19 pandemic, as it is able to track and diagnose computerized images (Ct‐scan) and chest X‐ray images \[[10](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0010)–[12](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0012)\]. Many science fiction films deal with this idea of integrating computers into human life, the most famous of which are *Transcendence*‐2014, *Her*‐2013, *A.I. AI*‐ 2001, *Ex Machina*‐ 2014. In this work, we will briefly and usefully address the three most significant topics that are widely circulated and applied in many fields: machine learning and deep learning, which are considered a large part of AI, also data science.

Evolutions in AI are somewhat complex and can be summarized in two concepts: machine learning and deep learning. These terms sometimes sound like buzzwords which are actually synonyms, which is why it is essential to know how to differentiate them and why they are so important in use. There are a lot of examples around; for instance, Netflix knows what movies and series TV you like to watch, Facebook knows who shows a destination in the photo, and both are reasons for self‐driving cars (Autonomous) [\[13\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0013). A customer service representative knows if you are satisfied with their service even before you fill out a customer satisfaction survey. This article focuses on finding out the difference between machine learning, deep learning, and data science.

 

## 10.2 Machine Learning

It is considered the most influential branch of AI and is responsible for designing algorithms that are able to learn and do not have to be explicitly programmed. In other words, the developer or programmer will not have to sit for hours to write the code or possible scenarios to produce updated algorithms. All that is required of him/her is to feed these algorithms with vast amounts of data so that the algorithms learn and know what they have to do and accomplish without any concern. There are two types of learning: supervised and unsupervised (see [Figure 10.1](#c10.xhtml#c10-fig-0001)).

Moreover, making a model in machine learning means designing a mathematical representation by generalizing and learning from the training data. Then, the designed machine learning model is involved in the new data to make predictions and earn effects. The model designed can be a regression model or a classification model based on the target variable known as the Y variable. Therefore, we must create a regression model if the target variable has a quantitative value. In contrast, if the data type of the target variable is qualitative, a classification model must be made. The absence of data can be a challenge when making machine learning models. The most vital metric of a classification machine learning model is accuracy. Besides, precision is the ratio of the total number of proper predictions to the total number of data points in the test dataset. In other words, accuracy is one way to evaluate a model's execution, but there are other ways. Nevertheless, accuracy offers the most reasonable perspective of a model's performance for a given dataset compared to different metrics. We employ metrics to evaluate the execution of a model. A model parameter is a value learned and evaluated during training from the dataset. The value must be approximated from the training data. Accordingly, the model parameters are internal variables of the machine learning model. Its values are set based on training. Machine learning models use these values to create predictions. It is based on the accuracy of model parameter values. Model parameter values have some degree of control over the sophistication of a model. The model complexity curve is a graphical texture employed to express the complexity of a model. It exhibits a relative increase in information as the example size increases. Also, it can be utilized for data deletion and speed up the learning process without concerning classification accuracy.

![Figure 10.1 Machine learning types. ¶ Source: Tkatek, Said et al. (2020)/Putting the world back to work: An expert system using big data and artificial intelligence in combating the spread of COVID‐19 and similar contagious diseases.](images/c10f001.jpg)

[**Figure 10.1**](#c10.xhtml#R_c10-fig-0001) Machine learning types. ¶ *Source:* Tkatek, Said et al. (2020)/Putting the world back to work: An expert system using big data and artificial intelligence in combating the spread of COVID‐19 and similar contagious diseases.

 

## 10.3 Deep Learning

Deep learning is a machine learning method applied in many domains as it is characterized by using many layers of nonlinear processing units to extract and transform features. In addition, each next layer takes the output from the previous layer as an input. Algorithms can be moderated (such as classification) or unsupervised (such as pattern analysis) \[[14](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0014)–[16](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0016)\]. Deep learning is an evolution machine learning model that utilizes a programmable neural network that allows machines to make sound judgments without human intervention. [Table 10.1](#c10.xhtml#c10-tbl-0001) illustrates the distinctions between machine learning and deep learning. Deep learning contains a set of architectures that rely heavily on learning and data representation [\[17\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0017). It supports the design of a hierarchical representation of data by deriving higher‐level properties from lower‐level properties. This representation learns multiple levels of representation that correspond to different various of abstraction. Moreover, the deep learning model depends mainly on learning from data representation. For example, when a representation of an image is mentioned, it can be considered as a vector of intensity values for each pixel or features such as edge sets and custom shapes. However, some of these features represent the data better. As a benefit of this scene, the deep learning model utilizes efficient algorithms to extract the hierarchical features that sufficiently represent the data rather than the handcrafted features. The deep learning model is leveraged to analyze data utilizing a logical structure that draws human‐like inferences. In fact, deep learning applications employ an artificial neural network to perform a specific action. The vision of artificial neural networks is inspired by the biological neural network of the human brain, which generates a learning process with much greater potential than those of traditional machine learning models.

[**Table 10.1**](#c10.xhtml#R_c10-tbl-0001)  The distinctions between machine learning and deep learning.

| Distinctions || Machine learning || Deep learning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dataset || Small to medium dataset || Big dataset |
| --- | --- | --- |
| H/W requirements || Low‐end machines || Requires machines with GPU |
| Engineering peculiarities || Understand the features of the data || Understand the functionality of the data |
| Training time || Short || Long |
| Processing time || Seconds or hours || Hours or weeks |
| Number of algorithm || Many || Few |
| Data interpretation || Easy and some algorithms are hardly || Difficult |

Deep learning concentrates on enhancing the AI process, which is where machines learn things. The core of deep learning lies in computers fast enough and enough data to train large neural networks. As a result, many companies employ deep learning and advanced AI to solve product issues and services. Some people say that deep learning is just another term for machine learning, but that's not proper. Deep learning is a subset of machine learning. People should stop trying to make machine learning/deep learning the solution to issues that could be solved more efficiently with straightforward math. Machine learning techniques have been utilized for a long time, but deep learning is far superior to its peers. Deep learning is effectively a way to perform pattern matching on a large scale. More significantly, deep learning has only had limited success in particular areas. Some specialists believe that reinforcement learning involves the development of AI models without providing them with an enormous amount of data labels. Deep reinforcement learning is one of the most exciting areas of AI investigation; it has limited sensation in solving real‐world issues.

 

## 10.4 Data Science

Data science can be expressed as a practice that aims to deal with all forms of data in terms of gathering, coordinating, analyzing, reviewing, and extracting the most critical features. This science integrates computer science fields and statistics with other modern sciences. Nowadays, data science is one of the most requested disciplines in the labor market because it is the basis on which many disciplines such as machine learning, deep learning and big data are based. It is the science that mainly supports the development of AI techniques. Companies seek to use data science techniques in analyzing their data and making decisions that help in expanding the business more broadly. Making decisions is not straightforward, so data science is a mixed and multidisciplinary education that includes mathematics, computer science, statistics, and business. The process of evidence analysis helps improve the quality of commercial, governmental, and administrative decision‐making and scientific research. The development of data science is being leveraged in many fields like the medical, military, agriculture and more. Data science provides significant insights into many complex matters, in many circumstances, with incredible accuracy and timeliness. Data science includes many vital and more complex processes that seek to predict, classify, or identify a pattern in the data or generate recommendations before taking a unique action. Data science is an interdisciplinary approach to finding and extracting multiple types of data by integrating analytical methods, specialist expertise, and modern technologies. This approach usually relies on the areas of data mining and forecasting using machine learning techniques, analysis of such predictions, statistics, and text analysis. In general, most organizations are short of experts to transform their big data to find insights and troubleshoot problems the organization is unaware of. To properly understand and monetize the value of data science, organizations will need to include forecasting in their data and improvement in their operational and business systems. Nowadays, many institutions seek to train specialized cadres in analyzing the data of their systems, who will contribute to the development and implementation of projects through machine learning techniques. In short, this field is in constant development, and we may hear a lot about it in the coming years. Data science includes illustrative, diagnostic, predictive, and educational capabilities, which indicates that with data science, organizations can employ data to learn what occurred, why it occurred, what will occur, and what they should do with the expected effect.

 

## 10.5 AI in the Agriculture Industry

 

### 10.5.1 Techniques in Agriculture

In this section, the significance of AI techniques in the agricultural industry and how modern technology contributes to the development of the agricultural sectors are reviewed. In this sector, many food producers suffer from threats and risks to their crops and the emergence of a group of diseases that damage crops. In general, the agricultural sector depends on natural forces in managing crops. In other words, the change in the nature of the climate and the lack of rainfall led to a shortage of workers available to work in this sector, which led to a decrease in agricultural production, especially in Iraq, which is considered one of the countries most affected by climate change and the lack of crops and the heavy dependence on imports from neighboring nations, which led to a significant increase in food prices. With all these challenges, the main issue that preoccupies the countries of the world is automation in the agricultural industry. The population is growing exponentially daily, and with the increase in population density, the necessity for food and work also increases daily. The methods used by agriculturalists are not sufficient to meet these requirements. Therefore, modern automated methods have been added to make things more straightforward and more productive. With the growth of AI techniques, applying this science in the agricultural sector has become vital. Scholars worldwide seek to conduct a set of research on using AI techniques in the agricultural industry. In the coming years, AI will have sufficient capacity to produce better predictive insights, as it will contribute significantly to increasing agricultural production while providing job opportunities for millions of people worldwide \[[18](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0018), [19](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0019)\].

There is a lot of literature in which deep learning techniques have been utilized in diagnosing and classifying crop diseases. In a study by Tiwari et al. [\[20\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0020), they advised applying a deep learning technique like the VGG‐19 model to investigate and detect potato leaf diseases. This study got an accuracy effect of more than 97%. In another study by Rangarajan et al. [\[21\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0021), they applied two deep learning‐based architectures, AlexNet and VGG‐16 net, to classify tomato crop disease. This study got the accuracy effects of both architectures at 97.29% and 97.49%, respectively. The agricultural industry can benefit from solutions powered by AI by managing environmental challenges using weather forecasts, establishing a soil and crop health monitoring system, and predictive analysis through the use of techniques that help collect information and data needed to make decisions about how to enhance production and also creating designs to detect rusts harmful to agriculture. In recent times, machine learning and deep learning techniques have become essential for the agricultural industry to rely on them to improve efficiency and production continuously. Moreover, these techniques help agriculturalists predict demand by analyzing information, knowing the population growth rate, and finding new areas that can be exploited to establish agricultural lands to keep pace with this population growth. Furthermore, these techniques help in the process of spraying and removing poison weeds and useless weeds through the use of drones (see [Figure 10.2](#c10.xhtml#c10-fig-0002)). Finally, these techniques help save excessive use of pesticides, water, and herbicides, maintain soil fertility, help in efficient use of labor and increase productivity while enhancing quality.

 

### 10.5.2 Smart Agriculture

In recent years, many governments have started to develop and apply smart agriculture, which refers to the application of modern and advanced technologies in the agricultural sector, as it comes to integrating the latest technological innovations with the aim of improving resources and making the most suitable decisions by obtaining accurate information and data about the state of agricultural products and the weather. Smart agriculture has become a significant trend that is rapidly developing thanks to the developments that help agriculturalists establish an advanced agricultural field, which is called precision agriculture. In addition, smart agriculture contributes to solving many of the problems faced by agriculturalists by relying on intelligent applications that contribute to increasing agricultural production by 70%, as expected in 2025 [\[23\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0023). This percentage may increase beyond 2030. [Figure 10.3](#c10.xhtml#c10-fig-0003) illustrates the use of drones to send agricultural data to agriculturalists' phones. Smart agriculture brings many benefits, some of which can be highlighted:

![Figure 10.2 Drones in agriculture sector. (a) Planting drone. (b) Irrigation drone. (c) Soil analysis drone. (d) Crop monitoring drone. (e) Crop spraying drone. (f) Health assessment drone. ¶ Source: From Casadiego \[22\], ORCID, CC0 1.0.](images/c10f002.jpg)

[**Figure 10.2**](#c10.xhtml#R_c10-fig-0002) Drones in agriculture sector. (a) Planting drone. (b) Irrigation drone. (c) Soil analysis drone. (d) Crop monitoring drone. (e) Crop spraying drone. (f) Health assessment drone. ¶ *Source:* From Casadiego [\[22\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0022), ORCID, CC0 1.0.

![Figure 10.3 Drones send agricultural data to agriculturalists' phones. ¶ Source: From Rehman et al. \[24\] / MDPI / CC BY 4.0.](images/c10f003.jpg)

[**Figure 10.3**](#c10.xhtml#R_c10-fig-0003) Drones send agricultural data to agriculturalists' phones. ¶ *Source:* From Rehman et al. [\[24\]](#c10.xhtml#c10-bib-0024) / MDPI / CC BY 4.0.

*   It contributes to increasing gains as new technologies allow to reduce the cost of resources such as water, energy or fertilizers and increase production with a higher quality that satisfies olives.
*   Full control of all planting‐related tasks and assist agriculturalists in completing duties in less time while supporting them make the right decisions.
*   It contributes to improving the quality of life for farmers and people working in the agricultural sector by integrating tools and applications and facilitating the process of control, incredibly remote control, without needing to be present in the field.
*   Smart applications are characterized by the automation of many aspects of the agricultural sector and agricultural production, as they save time and costs.
*   Agricultural resolutions based on AI are highly sustainable, as these applications seek to make more sustainable and highly efficient operations.

Big data is one of the things that contribute to the growth of the agricultural sector with AI. Moreover, agriculture has begun to integrate advanced applications that allow the extraction of data, statistics, and numbers increasingly, by extracting effects and determining the type of agriculture that is most productive and in demand by the consumer to meet future needs. Where smart sensors are employed to obtain soil, water, weather, or plant data and deliver these details to the grower's phone efficiently, with graphs illustrating daily agricultural activity, the use of robots and drones is expected to increase by 2030 in agricultural tasks to extract high‐quality data with high accuracy and trustworthiness (see [Figure 10.4](#c10.xhtml#c10-fig-0004)). These drones help in the continuous monitoring of agricultural crops and the detection of potential problems in less time and lower costs, such as controlling pests and preventing the spread of diseases among plants. On the one hand, AI contributes to the creation of applications that allow remote integration of the various elements in the agricultural sector, advanced software to govern and manage the agricultural production process, with the significant development of agricultural machines that employ the global positioning system (GPS) and can be handled through a smartphone. Automation is considered one of the main elements in the process of transition to smart agriculture, where various aspects and elements are automated during the stages of agricultural production based on previous data and records that are made by tools such as smart sensors. For illustration, the automation of irrigation or the weather allows the achievement of suitable climatic conditions for the development of plant production. AI has a significant and influential role in the agricultural sector, as it continuously seeks to develop machines and tools that help collect crops, predict weather conditions, and provide more efficient systems. Precision agriculture is an AI solution in the agricultural industry. This technology is applied in the health monitoring of plants, field harvesting, weed and pest control, detection of soil shortages, and other tasks that benefit the agriculturalist. Without a doubt, smart agriculture still has a lot to do to reach a very advanced stage and contribute to facilitating decision‐making and improving the quality of agricultural crops and the use of smart sensors to monitor changes in climate, water, or soil and control them remotely through a smartphone or laptop or from any other device connected to the internet.

![Figure 10.4 Involve robots in agricultural production. (a) sompong\_tom/Adobe Stock Photos. (b) kinwun/Adobe Stock Photos. (c) Pugun & Photo Studio/Adobe Stock Photos.](images/c10f004.jpg)

[**Figure 10.4**](#c10.xhtml#R_c10-fig-0004) Involve robots in agricultural production. (a) sompong\_tom/Adobe Stock Photos. (b) kinwun/Adobe Stock Photos. (c) Pugun & Photo Studio/Adobe Stock Photos.

Smart agriculture involves information and data technologies to optimize complicated agricultural systems. The priority is data access and how producers can employ the information organized intelligently. The technology employed in smart agriculture ranges from IoT and robotics to drones and AI. With these implements, agriculturalists can monitor farm essentials without needing on‐site physical existence. This allows them to make decisions for the entire area, a lot, or even a single plant. However, smart agriculture is not just for large agriculturalists but can also be utilized by small agriculturists, organic growers, or other smaller‐scale operations. The entire smart agriculture technique is handled by software and monitored by sensors. In addition, automation has allowed influential gains in production efficiency, quality gains, and sustainability. One of the challenges of smart agriculture is the interoperability between all the techniques and the different digital initiatives to encourage access to the data generated. As the complexity of agriculture increases, so does the number of parties interested and the sophistication of information flows. Current production techniques employ machines and software from multiple manufacturers. As a result, producers must deal with many proprietary data formats, code lists, etc. Thus, standardized interfaces and data formats are needed to gain interoperability. This interconnection suggests information interactions between manufacturers of inputs for production, distributors, producers, the food industry, and service providers. Also, even clients are included, who increasingly make purchasing decisions based on the availability of such information. In general, the purpose of employing information and communication technology, which is based on getting data, analyzing it, and utilizing it to optimize processes and decision‐making, is to enhance the economic effects for the producer, reduce the risks of a specific activity, and encourage increasingly sustainable techniques.

 

## 10.6 Conclusions

AI techniques have a significant and distinct role in many different industries, such as transportation, finance, health care, and now agriculture. In addition, it has helped agriculturalists monitor their agricultural products with considerable advantages in spreading their crops. Moreover, AI in agriculture not only helps agriculturalists to automate their cultivation but also leads to precision farming for better crop yield and more excellent quality while using fewer resources. AI employs modern technologies that help agriculturalists monitor their crops without needing individual farm monitoring. Many companies and startups today expect to develop AI in agriculture. AI in agriculture is redefining the traditional pattern of farming. The future of AI in agriculture has become progressive in developing overall transformation using various advanced strategies.

# 11  
An Overview of Predictive Maintenance and Load Forecasting

*Nand Kishor Gupta¹, Vivek Upadhyaya¹, and Vijay Gali²*

*¹ Department of Electrical & Electronics Engineering, Poornima University, Jaipur, India*

*² Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering, Nazarbayev University, Astana, Kazakhstan*

 

## 11.1 Introduction

With the advent of predictive maintenance (PdM) and load forecasting, the old adage that “prevention is better than cure” has taken on a whole new meaning in the realm of industrial operations and the distribution of energy. The asset management, maintenance methods, and energy optimization practices of many businesses will need to undergo a paradigm shift as a result of these two interconnected areas. More and more, automation is making our lives easier. Data‐driven insights are very helpful for ensuring important machines last longer, lowering downtime, and improving business efficiency.

Most maintenance practices in the past have been planned or reactive, which has led to breaks that were not expected, costly repairs, and production stops. The creation of PdM, on the contrary, has made it possible to be more proactive. The Internet of Things (IoT), data analytics, and machine learning are all used in PdM to figure out when things will break. At the same time, load forecasting has evolved as an essential component of energy management. This provides utilities with the ability to predict customer demand patterns and optimize energy generation and distribution.

This chapter will begin an in‐depth investigation of the interconnected fields of PdM and load forecasting. We are going to go into the fundamental ideas, approaches, benefits, and challenges, as well as the real‐world applications, that highlight the significance of these things in the contemporary industrial and energy scene. It is essential for engineers, academics, managers, and politicians alike to have a comprehensive understanding of PdM and load forecasting as automation continues to have a profound impact on the business world and the electrical systems that support it. This chapter is divided into three sections, which are outlined in [Figure 11.1](#c11.xhtml#c11-fig-0001).

Both the manufacturing and energy industries throughout the world have seen significant benefits from implementing PdM and load forecasting. Because of their ability to foresee impending failure, maximize the effectiveness of resource allocation, and enhance the rate at which energy is distributed, the future will be more sustainable and robust thanks to their contributions. To achieve levels of operational excellence and energy sustainability that have never been seen before, it is essential to tap into the potential of PdM and load forecasting. This is the key to unlocking levels of operational excellence and energy sustainability that have never been seen before. In this chapter, the complexities of various domains are broken down, paving the way for a complete knowledge of the ramifications of those domains and encouraging creativity in an era driven by data and automation.

 

## 11.2 PdM: Revolutionizing Asset Management

The revolutionary approach to asset management known as PdM makes use of data analytics, machine learning, and the IoT to alter the traditional maintenance practices that have been used up until now. Businesses in a wide range of fields can use this cutting‐edge way to make their operations more efficient, cut down on downtime, save money, and make their most valuable assets last longer. This part of the piece will talk about the ideas, benefits, technology, and real‐world uses of PdM that make it such a big deal in asset management.

 

### 11.2.1 Development of Maintenance Procedures

Maintenance practices have changed over time, which has had a huge impact on how businesses run. As this journey has gone on, there has been a shift from defensive strategies to proactive ones. The way maintenance was done in the past was reactive, which means that problems with tools were fixed after they happened. This caused downtime, higher fixed costs, and problems with how things worked. This section looks at how maintenance processes have changed over time and focuses on the important steps that had to be taken before PdM became a paradigm that changed everything. In particular, the history of upkeep practices is looked at. A guess at the main steps that make up the maintenance method is shown in [Figure 11.2](#c11.xhtml#c11-fig-0002).

 ![Figure 11.1 Chapter organization.](images/c11f001.jpg)

[**Figure 11.1**](#c11.xhtml#R_c11-fig-0001) Chapter organization.

 ![Figure 11.2 Basic stages of maintenance process.](images/c11f002.jpg)

[**Figure 11.2**](#c11.xhtml#R_c11-fig-0002) Basic stages of maintenance process.

 

#### 11.2.1.1 Reactive Maintenance

This first stage was different because it used a “break‐fix” approach, which meant that preventative maintenance procedures were carried out when pieces of equipment broke down. Even though it fixed problems quickly, reactive maintenance led to longer periods of downtime, less work getting done, and operations that were hard to plan for.

 

#### 11.2.1.2 Preventive Maintenance

The next step meant setting up regular maintenance tasks that would be done at set times or based on usage factors. It was the goal of this plan to cut down on breakdowns by replacing or maintaining equipment before it broke down. Even though downtime was cut down a lot, preventative maintenance was not always accurate, and parts were often replaced when they were not needed.

 

#### 11.2.1.3 Predictive Maintenance

With the rise of data‐driven insights, advanced analytics, and the IoT, predictive repair became possible. This preventative method uses sensor data, real‐time tracking, and predictive algorithms to figure out when the piece of equipment will break down. PdM looks at recurring patterns and outliers to make maintenance plans more effective. This saves money, cuts down on downtime, and makes technology last longer.

The evolution of maintenance practices from reactive to predictive represents a key transformation in industrial operations. PdM's reliance on real‐time data, advanced analytics and predictive algorithms enables industries to move from a reactive to a proactive stance, thereby optimizing asset management, reducing costs and ensuring operational continuity. As technology advances, PdM is poised to play a central role in shaping the future of maintenance strategies across sectors.

The concept of multilevel anomaly detection has been introduced in prior research [\[1\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0001). The initial stage involves the utilization of the Boruta algorithm [\[2\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0002) to process the data, thereby extracting the most significant features from the unprocessed data. The second phase involves the utilization of fire partitioning [\[3\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0003) in conjunction with the k‐medoid partitioning algorithm [\[4\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0004) to ascertain the optimal number of clusters. The third phase involves employing a DBSCAN‐based algorithm for the purpose of identifying and classifying potentially suspicious objects. The technique does not enforce the aggregation of all data points into a single cluster, hence resulting in infrequent identification of a distinct cluster of nonhuman data points that can be classified as abnormal. The proposed method has undergone testing on six distinct datasets sourced from online databases. The obtained findings demonstrate enhanced performance in many detection metrics, including the F1 score, true positive rate, false positive rate, and detection rate. Furthermore, with the DBSCAN algorithm, there is no requirement to explicitly specify beginning parameters as the method itself is designed to determine these values during the calculation process.

The authors presented a two‐stage unsupervised approach for the detection of anomalous data using acoustic sensors [\[5\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0005). Initially, the researchers partitioned the acoustic data into temporal intervals and subsequently employed three distinct techniques, namely, linear predictive coding (LPC) [\[6\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0006), mel frequency cepstral coefficients (MFCC) [\[7\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0007), and Gamma Transition (GMCC) [\[8\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0008), to extract features from the unprocessed data. The initial stage involves employing the BIRCH method to partition the data into many microclusters, utilizing the computed features as the basis for division. In the second stage, the merging of groups is performed based on the distance between centroids. Specifically, if the distance between two centroids is found to be below a predetermined threshold value, the merge operation is executed. Conversely, if the distance exceeds the threshold, the groups are considered to be nonmutually exclusive and are not merged. The ultimate result comprises two distinct categories: the first category encompasses action points, while the second category encompasses unusual events. In addition to the inclusion of adverse stimuli, examinations are administered to gather contextual information through the utilization of auditory cues such as gunshots, shattered glass, sirens, and vocal expressions of distress. The approach demonstrated favorable outcomes, as evidenced by measurement accuracy, memory, and F1 score surpassing 90%. The F1 score and accuracy index exhibit superior performance compared with the one‐step approach that solely relies on the macro clustering method.

The authors in reference [\[9\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0009) introduced a decentralized architecture wherein the task of short‐term anomaly detection is carried out at sensor nodes via an unsupervised artificial neural network (ANN) algorithm. During this stage, the data collected from the sensor nodes is consolidated and analyzed to enable seamless integration of the information. The subsequent phase will manifest within the cloud infrastructure and is expected to be promptly detected. Examine the association between sensors that exhibit a high degree of correlation. Hence, whereas short‐term analysis entails assessing the likelihood of uncertainty and the temporal span during which data will be impacted, cloud computing necessitates a lengthier duration and relies on past data to determine sensor replacement and anomaly detection. It should be noted that the authors employ restricted Boltzmann machines (RBMs) to leverage the notion of creative games [\[10\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0010) and specifically to train ANN techniques in a cloud computing environment, considering the limiting capabilities of sensor points. Furthermore, it should be noted that the sensor node does not keep any data on its edge. This implies that not only the algorithm but also other relevant information is not written on the device. The authors of [\[11\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0011) offer a system for tracking and distributing. The solution's architecture comprises a collection of low‐power devices and heads, which are energy‐efficient devices positioned in proximity to the sensor nodes. The detection model was initially constructed using principal component analysis (PCA) \[[12](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0012), [13](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0013)\] in the offline training phase to compute the eigenvectors and eigenvalue matrices of the training data. At this juncture, the calculation of parameter inequality is performed for each point, utilizing the eigenvectors and eigenvalues matrix. The process of negative detection involves identifying variable values that fall beyond the established calculation boundaries during the course of the investigation. The averaging of data by the sensor points facilitates the generation of new patterns through the transmission of data to the board. The board then utilizes the data obtained from many sensor points to construct novel global models, which are subsequently transmitted back to the nodes. The efficacy of the approach was evaluated through the generation of synthetic objects and the subsequent input of data utilizing the sensor network provided by the Intel Berkeley Research Laboratory (IBRL). The findings indicate that the integration of local and global defect detection models yields superior outcomes in comparison to solely employing local models. When the approach was juxtaposed with the distribution of findings derived from time–space relationships, the outcomes indicated that just one way exhibited superior performance in terms of value analysis, while the measurement system was deemed inaccurate. However, the authors discovered that this particular method lacked comprehensiveness.

 

### 11.2.2 Data‐Driven Insights for Proactive Decision‐Making

Particularly in the context of PdM and optimization techniques, the era of data‐driven insights has brought about a new paradigm for proactive decision‐making in sectors. This section goes deeper into the revolutionary impact of data‐driven insights, which are made possible by the IoT, big data analytics, and machine learning algorithms. The end goal of this section is to attain proactive and informed decision‐making.

 

#### 11.2.2.1 Role of IoT and Sensor Networks

The IoT has completely altered the processes of data collection and transmission. A continuous flow of data is produced as a result of the continuous collection of real‐time information by sensors that are incorporated into machines, equipment, and processes. These sensor networks enable the monitoring of factors, such as temperature, pressure, vibration, and performance metrics, and they provide a comprehensive perspective of the operating circumstances.

 

#### 11.2.2.2 Big Data Analytics and Machine Learning

Tools for sophisticated analytics are necessary because of the sheer volume and complexity of the data provided by IoT devices. Big data analytics tools, when combined with machine learning algorithms, sort through this data to find patterns, anomalies, and trends that might not be seen using more conventional methods. These algorithms learn from previous data and modify their behavior in response to changing situations, which results in improved forecast accuracy.

 

#### 11.2.2.3 Predictive Algorithms

Data‐driven insights enable the creation of predictive algorithms that identify early signs of equipment degradation or impending failures. By comparing real‐time data with historical records, these algorithms recognize deviations from normal operating patterns. This proactive approach allows maintenance teams to intervene before failure occurs, minimizing downtime and optimizing resource allocation.

 

#### 11.2.2.4 Real‐Time Monitoring and Alerts

The integration of real‐time monitoring with data analytics empowers industries to receive instant alerts when anomalies are detected. Maintenance teams can respond promptly, scheduling maintenance activities based on actual equipment conditions rather than generic time intervals.

 

#### 11.2.2.5 Benefits of Data‐Driven Insights

The adoption of data‐driven insights for proactive decision‐making offers numerous advantages. By identifying issues early, industries can avoid catastrophic failures, optimize maintenance schedules, extend equipment lifespans, and enhance operational efficiency. Moreover, predictive insights contribute to a reduction in operational costs and unplanned downtime.

 

#### 11.2.2.6 Limitations and Challenges

Despite its potential, leveraging data‐driven insights poses challenges. Ensuring data quality, managing data integration across various systems, and training accurate machine learning models require dedicated efforts. Organizations must also address concerns related to data privacy, security, and the interpretability of complex algorithms.

With the data‐driven method, learning is based on data gathered from sensors and actuators in the factory. The spread of technologies, such as IIoT and CPS [\[14\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0014), has made this method even more important. The gathered information can be used to look into how a part breaks down or to make a model that can guess its RUL. In recent years, researchers have looked into the data‐driven approach's promise in the age of “big data.” This is because it can be used with other methods in a number of different model approaches. Using statistics or machine learning, the collected data can be used to make a behavioral model or get the physical model of a certain expression, for instance. The multilayered system can handle the complexity of today's business world and use the data from older systems. This is possible because old data can be mixed with the standard for data‐driven systems to make the method work better.

Some ways to handle data, such as visual search, statistics, and machine learning, can be broken down into two groups. Many models and methods for PdM have been written about, such as the Hidden Markov Model (HMM) [\[15\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0015), the Wiener Process Model (WPM) [\[16\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0016), the Gamma Process Model [\[17\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0017), the Proportional Hazards Model [\[18\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0018), and the Autoregressive Model. ARMA, which stands for equivalent linear moving average model [\[19\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0019). A number of machine learning techniques are used, such as ANN \[[20](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0020), [21](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0021)\] and its variations, SVM [\[22\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0022), RF [\[23\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0023), xGBoost [\[24\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0024), and SOM [\[25\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0025). As technologies, such as IIoT and CPS [\[14\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0014), have become more common, this method has become even more important. The gathered data can be used to look into how parts break down or to make behavioral models from the data and figure out their RUL. In recent years, a lot of research has been done on the promise of data‐driven approaches in the age of “big data.” This is mostly because these approaches can be used with other approaches in a “multimodel” way. For instance, statistical or machine learning techniques can be used on the collected data to make a model of how a system works or a physical model of a certain part. The multilayered system can handle the complexity of today's business world and use the data from older systems. This is possible because old data can be mixed with the standard for data‐driven systems to make the method work better.

Some ways to handle data, such as visual search, statistics, and machine learning, can be broken down into two groups. In the research field, there are many models and methods for PdM. These include the HMM [\[15\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0015), the WPM [\[16\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0016), the Gamma Process Model [\[17\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0017), the Proportional Hazards Model [\[18\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0018), and the Autoregressive Model. ARMA, which stands for equivalent linear moving average model [\[19\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0019). Several machine learning techniques are used, such as ANN \[[20](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0020), [21](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0021)\] and its variations, SVM [\[22\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0022), RF [\[23\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0023), xGBoost [\[24\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0024), and SOM [\[25\]](#c11.xhtml#c11-bib-0025). Data‐driven insights have reshaped decision‐making from a reactive to a proactive stance, particularly in the context of PdM. The marriage of IoT, big data analytics, and machine learning empowers industries to harness real‐time information, identify patterns, and predict potential equipment failures. As technology continues to evolve, the role of data‐driven insights in facilitating proactive decision‐making will remain pivotal in ensuring efficient, reliable, and resilient industrial operations.
